People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi, Dr. [Name]. Thank you for fitting me in. Well, I came in today because... gosh, where do I even start? I just seem to worry about absolutely everything now. And I mean everything. 

I’m a real estate broker, and, you know, you always have a little stress with the job, but lately my worry about my clients and the housing market—which, granted, is a bit all over the place right now with the rates—but my reaction to it just feels completely out of proportion to what is actually happening. I know the business, I’ve been doing this a long time, but I just can't stop fretting over every little detail. 

And it’s not just work, it spills over into everything else. I worry about my family constantly, I worry about money even though we're okay, it's... oh, even this condo! We recently moved to a condo, downsizing, you know, which was supposed to make life simpler, but I just agonize over whether it was the right decision. I'll sit there and think, 'Did we make a mistake? What if it doesn't appreciate? Did we sell the house at the wrong time?' It’s just exhausting. I know it’s too much, I know I shouldn't be this wound up over things that are mostly fine, but my brain just won't let it go and I just don't know how to turn it off anymore.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, not exactly. I mean, I don't have one specific thought that just loops like a broken record, and I certainly don't do things over and over again to make it stop. I know some people have to, you know, check the locks a dozen times or wash their hands constantly, but it’s nothing like that for me. It’s not a specific action I have to perform. 

Although, I suppose my husband would say I check my phone for work emails too much, but honestly, in real estate, especially right now, if you miss a text from a buyer's agent, you could lose the whole deal, so that's just being responsible. That's just the job. But as for the thoughts... it's not one single recurring thought, it's more like a domino effect. It just travels from one thing to the next. 

Like with the condo, I'll start out wondering if we got a good interest rate on the mortgage, and then suddenly I'm worrying about the building's HOA reserves, and then that makes me worry about our own personal retirement accounts, and then I'm stressing over whether my daughter is saving enough in her 401k... it just spirals. It’s never just one thought stuck in my head, it's a whole parade of different worries. And I don't do anything to try and reduce it because, well, I don't even know what I *could* do to stop it. It just keeps flowing from one topic to another all day long, completely on its own.

I'll sit there and think, 'Did we make a mistake? What if it doesn't appreciate? Did we sell the house at the wrong time?' It’s just exhausting. I know it’s too much, I know I shouldn't be this wound up over things that are mostly fine, but my brain just won't let it go and I just don't know how to turn it off anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any recurring thoughts that you can’t seem to get out of your head, or do you find yourself doing certain things over and over again to try to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: No, not exactly. I mean, I don't have one specific thought that just loops like a broken record, and I certainly don't do things over and over again to make it stop. I know some people have to, you know, check the locks a dozen times or wash their hands constantly, but it’s nothing like that for me. It’s not a specific action I have to perform. \n\nAlthough, I suppose my husband would say I check my phone for work emails too much, but honestly, in real estate, especially right now, if you miss a text from a buyer's agent, you could lose the whole deal, so that's just being responsible. That's just the job. But as for the thoughts... it's not one single recurring thought, it's more like a domino effect. It just travels from one thing to the next. \n\nLike with the condo, I'll start out wondering if we got a good interest rate on the mortgage, and then suddenly I'm worrying about the building's HOA reserves, and then that makes me worry about our own personal retirement accounts, and then I'm stressing over whether my daughter is saving enough in her 401k... it just spirals. It’s never just one thought stuck in my head, it's a whole parade of different worries. And I don't do anything to try and reduce it because, well, I don't even know what I *could* do to stop it. Well, yes, actually. The sleeping part is... it's just awful. You know, I try to do everything right. I make sure I'm in bed at a reasonable hour, usually by 10:30 or 11:00, because I know how important rest is, especially at my age, and especially when you have to be "on" for clients all day. But getting into bed and actually sleeping are two very different things for me lately. 

I’ll lay there, and it’s like my brain just clicks into overdrive the second the lights go out. I’ll start thinking about the condo fees—because they just raised them again, and I wonder if they're going to keep going up and if we should have factored that in more—and then my mind jumps to the showings I have the next day. I'll start mentally walking through the properties, wondering if the staging is right, if I should have brought fresh flowers for the kitchen island, if I missed some tiny clause in a contract I sent over for signatures earlier that afternoon... 

It just circles and circles, and I'm lying there staring at the ceiling for what feels like hours. It takes me so long to finally settle down and drift off. And even when I do finally fall asleep, I don't think it's good sleep, you know? Because when my alarm goes off, I wake up feeling completely drained, like I never fully rested at all. I drag myself out of bed feeling just as exhausted as when I got in it. It’s like my brain is running a marathon while I’m supposed to be recharging. So yes, the sleep is definitely a big problem.

I know the business, I’ve been doing this a long time, but I just can't stop fretting over every little detail. \n\nAnd it’s not just work, it spills over into everything else. I worry about my family constantly, I worry about money even though we're okay, it's... oh, even this condo! We recently moved to a condo, downsizing, you know, which was supposed to make life simpler, but I just agonize over whether it was the right decision. I'll sit there and think, 'Did we make a mistake? What if it doesn't appreciate? Did we sell the house at the wrong time?' It’s just exhausting. I know it’s too much, I know I shouldn't be this wound up over things that are mostly fine, but my brain just won't let it go and I just don't know how to turn it off anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any recurring thoughts that you can’t seem to get out of your head, or do you find yourself doing certain things over and over again to try to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: No, not exactly. I mean, I don't have one specific thought that just loops like a broken record, and I certainly don't do things over and over again to make it stop. I know some people have to, you know, check the locks a dozen times or wash their hands constantly, but it’s nothing like that for me. It’s not a specific action I have to perform. \n\nAlthough, I suppose my husband would say I check my phone for work emails too much, but honestly, in real estate, especially right now, if you miss a text from a buyer's agent, you could lose the whole deal, so that's just being responsible. That's just the job. But as for the thoughts... it's not one single recurring thought, it's more like a domino effect. It just travels from one thing to the next. \n\nLike with the condo, I'll start out wondering if we got a good interest rate on the mortgage, and then suddenly I'm worrying about the building's HOA reserves, and then that makes me worry about our own personal retirement accounts, and then I'm stressing over whether my daughter is saving enough in her 401k... it just spirals. It’s never just one thought stuck in my head, it's a whole parade of different worries. And I don't do anything to try and reduce it because, well, I don't even know what I *could* do to stop it. It just keeps flowing from one topic to another all day long, completely on its own.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any physical symptoms like restlessness, trouble sleeping, muscle tension, or irritability that seem to go along with all this worrying?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, actually. The sleeping part is... it's just awful. You know, I try to do everything right. I make sure I'm in bed at a reasonable hour, usually by 10:30 or 11:00, because I know how important rest is, especially at my age, and especially when you have to be \"on\" for clients all day. But getting into bed and actually sleeping are two very different things for me lately. \n\nI’ll lay there, and it’s like my brain just clicks into overdrive the second the lights go out. I’ll start thinking about the condo fees—because they just raised them again, and I wonder if they're going to keep going up and if we should have factored that in more—and then my mind jumps to the showings I have the next day. I'll start mentally walking through the properties, wondering if the staging is right, if I should have brought fresh flowers for the kitchen island, if I missed some tiny clause in a contract I sent over for signatures earlier that afternoon... \n\nIt just circles and circles, and I'm lying there staring at the ceiling for what feels like hours. It takes me so long to finally settle down and drift off. And even when I do finally fall asleep, I don't think it's good sleep, you know? Because when my alarm goes off, I wake up feeling completely drained, like I never fully rested at all. I drag myself out of bed feeling just as exhausted as when I got in it. It’s like my brain is running a marathon while I’m supposed to be recharging. So yes, the sleep is definitely a big problem.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, let me think... it's been quite a while now. I'd say it really started about a year and two months ago. Yes, right around then. That's when we finally sold our house—the one we'd lived in for over thirty years, where we raised our daughter, you know? It was a beautiful place, a big colonial, but it was just getting to be too much upkeep for the two of us, especially with my husband's knees acting up, and the yard work was endless. 

So we decided to downsize and move into this condo. Which, at the time, seemed like the most sensible thing in the world. We found a lovely unit, great natural light, close to downtown... but almost the minute the ink was dry on the closing papers, this whole... cloud of worry just descended on me. 

I remember the first few nights in the new place, surrounded by all those boxes. I should have been relieved that the move was over, but instead, I was just lying awake, staring at an unfamiliar ceiling, second-guessing everything. Did we get enough for the house? Did we overpay for the condo? Are the neighbors going to be noisy? What if the building has structural issues they didn't disclose? And it just never stopped. It started with the move, about fourteen months ago, and instead of settling down once we got unpacked, the worrying just sort of... expanded. It latched onto the move first, and then it just kept finding new things to attach itself to, right up until today.

I know it’s too much, I know I shouldn't be this wound up over things that are mostly fine, but my brain just won't let it go and I just don't know how to turn it off anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any recurring thoughts that you can’t seem to get out of your head, or do you find yourself doing certain things over and over again to try to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: No, not exactly. I mean, I don't have one specific thought that just loops like a broken record, and I certainly don't do things over and over again to make it stop. I know some people have to, you know, check the locks a dozen times or wash their hands constantly, but it’s nothing like that for me. It’s not a specific action I have to perform. \n\nAlthough, I suppose my husband would say I check my phone for work emails too much, but honestly, in real estate, especially right now, if you miss a text from a buyer's agent, you could lose the whole deal, so that's just being responsible. It started with the move, about fourteen months ago, and instead of settling down once we got unpacked, the worrying just sort of... expanded. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, no, not really. I mean, I wouldn't say I feel hopeless or down in the dumps like that. It's not a sadness, if that makes sense. It's more of a... a constant buzzing energy, but not the good kind of energy. It's that nervous, on-edge feeling, like I'm waiting for the other shoe to drop all the time. 

I certainly haven't lost interest in things. I still love my job, even though the market is just completely unpredictable right now—one minute houses are sitting for sixty days, and the next, there's a bidding war on a complete fixer-upper. I still enjoy taking my clients out, showing them properties, negotiating the deals. In fact, sometimes I think I throw myself into the work *too* much because it gives my brain something specific to focus on, even if I end up worrying about the contracts later. 

And I still love seeing my daughter, of course, and going out to dinner with my husband. We have this little Italian place we go to every Friday night, and I still look forward to that. We've been going there for fifteen years, they know exactly how I like my chicken piccata... So, no, I haven't lost interest in my life or felt hopeless for weeks on end. It’s just that I can’t seem to enjoy any of those things fully because there's always this underlying current of worry running through my mind while I'm doing them. I'm sitting there at dinner, eating my chicken, and part of my brain is wondering if I remembered to lock the patio door at the condo, or if the HOA is going to send out another special assessment notice. But no, no deep depression or hopelessness like you're describing. Just the worrying.

It was a beautiful place, a big colonial, but it was just getting to be too much upkeep for the two of us, especially with my husband's knees acting up, and the yard work was endless. \n\nSo we decided to downsize and move into this condo. Which, at the time, seemed like the most sensible thing in the world. We found a lovely unit, great natural light, close to downtown... but almost the minute the ink was dry on the closing papers, this whole... cloud of worry just descended on me. \n\nI remember the first few nights in the new place, surrounded by all those boxes. I should have been relieved that the move was over, but instead, I was just lying awake, staring at an unfamiliar ceiling, second-guessing everything. Did we get enough for the house? Did we overpay for the condo? Are the neighbors going to be noisy? What if the building has structural issues they didn't disclose? And it just never stopped. It started with the move, about fourteen months ago, and instead of settling down once we got unpacked, the worrying just sort of... expanded. It latched onto the move first, and then it just kept finding new things to attach itself to, right up until today.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days where you felt down, hopeless, or lost interest in things you usually enjoy, for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks at a time?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I mean, I wouldn't say I feel hopeless or down in the dumps like that. It's not a sadness, if that makes sense. It's more of a... a constant buzzing energy, but not the good kind of energy. It's that nervous, on-edge feeling, like I'm waiting for the other shoe to drop all the time. \n\nI certainly haven't lost interest in things. I still love my job, even though the market is just completely unpredictable right now—one minute houses are sitting for sixty days, and the next, there's a bidding war on a complete fixer-upper. I still enjoy taking my clients out, showing them properties, negotiating the deals. In fact, sometimes I think I throw myself into the work *too* much because it gives my brain something specific to focus on, even if I end up worrying about the contracts later. \n\nAnd I still love seeing my daughter, of course, and going out to dinner with my husband. We have this little Italian place we go to every Friday night, and I still look forward to that. We've been going there for fifteen years, they know exactly how I like my chicken piccata... So, no, I haven't lost interest in my life or felt hopeless for weeks on end. It’s just that I can’t seem to enjoy any of those things fully because there's always this underlying current of worry running through my mind while I'm doing them. I'm sitting there at dinner, eating my chicken, and part of my brain is wondering if I remembered to lock the patio door at the condo, or if the HOA is going to send out another special assessment notice. But no, no deep depression or hopelessness like you're describing. Just the worrying.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Well, yes. Yes, I do. That's exactly why I'm here, actually, because it feels like I worry about absolutely everything now, even when I'm just sitting on the sofa and nothing is technically wrong. It's just constant. I'll be sitting there, and suddenly I'm worrying about my clients, you know? Did I price that new listing correctly? Should we have painted the trim before putting it on the market? And then that spirals into worrying about the real estate market in general, because interest rates are doing whatever they're doing, and who knows if buyers are going to dry up completely by spring. 

And then, if it's not work, it's my family. I worry about my daughter, even though she's thirty-two and perfectly capable, but I'll wonder if she's saving enough for retirement or if she's working too many hours. And money—oh, I worry about money constantly, even though we made a good profit on the house. I just sit there and think, what if inflation eats away at our savings? What if my husband's knees get worse and he needs surgery and Medicare doesn't cover all the physical therapy? 

And of course, there's the condo. I'm still second-guessing if moving was the right decision, even on a quiet Sunday morning when nothing is happening. I'll just be drinking my coffee, and my mind is racing through all these scenarios, and... and I know, logically, that it's out of proportion. I know I'm making mountains out of molehills, but I just can't seem to stop the wheels from turning. It's like my brain is always searching for a problem to solve, even when there isn't one right in front of me.

I know the business, I’ve been doing this a long time, but I just can't stop fretting over every little detail. \n\nAnd it’s not just work, it spills over into everything else. I'll sit there and think, 'Did we make a mistake? What if it doesn't appreciate? Did we sell the house at the wrong time?' It’s just exhausting. I know it’s too much, I know I shouldn't be this wound up over things that are mostly fine, but my brain just won't let it go and I just don't know how to turn it off anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any recurring thoughts that you can’t seem to get out of your head, or do you find yourself doing certain things over and over again to try to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: No, not exactly. I mean, I don't have one specific thought that just loops like a broken record, and I certainly don't do things over and over again to make it stop. I know some people have to, you know, check the locks a dozen times or wash their hands constantly, but it’s nothing like that for me. It’s not a specific action I have to perform. \n\nAlthough, I suppose my husband would say I check my phone for work emails too much, but honestly, in real estate, especially right now, if you miss a text from a buyer's agent, you could lose the whole deal, so that's just being responsible. That's just the job. But as for the thoughts... it's not one single recurring thought, it's more like a domino effect. It just travels from one thing to the next. \n\nLike with the condo, I'll start out wondering if we got a good interest rate on the mortgage, and then suddenly I'm worrying about the building's HOA reserves, and then that makes me worry about our own personal retirement accounts, and then I'm stressing over whether my daughter is saving enough in her 401k... it just spirals. It’s never just one thought stuck in my head, it's a whole parade of different worries. And I don't do anything to try and reduce it because, well, I don't even know what I *could* do to stop it. It just keeps flowing from one topic to another all day long, completely on its own.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any physical symptoms like restlessness, trouble sleeping, muscle tension, or irritability that seem to go along with all this worrying?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, actually. The sleeping part is... it's just awful. You know, I try to do everything right. I make sure I'm in bed at a reasonable hour, usually by 10:30 or 11:00, because I know how important rest is, especially at my age, and especially when you have to be \"on\" for clients all day. But getting into bed and actually sleeping are two very different things for me lately. \n\nI’ll lay there, and it’s like my brain just clicks into overdrive the second the lights go out. I’ll start thinking about the condo fees—because they just raised them again, and I wonder if they're going to keep going up and if we should have factored that in more—and then my mind jumps to the showings I have the next day. I'll start mentally walking through the properties, wondering if the staging is right, if I should have brought fresh flowers for the kitchen island, if I missed some tiny clause in a contract I sent over for signatures earlier that afternoon... \n\nIt just circles and circles, and I'm lying there staring at the ceiling for what feels like hours. It takes me so long to finally settle down and drift off. And even when I do finally fall asleep, I don't think it's good sleep, you know? Because when my alarm goes off, I wake up feeling completely drained, like I never fully rested at all. I drag myself out of bed feeling just as exhausted as when I got in it. It’s like my brain is running a marathon while I’m supposed to be recharging. So yes, the sleep is definitely a big problem.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with constant worrying and trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think... it's been quite a while now. That's when we finally sold our house—the one we'd lived in for over thirty years, where we raised our daughter, you know? Did we overpay for the condo? Are the neighbors going to be noisy? What if the building has structural issues they didn't disclose? And it just never stopped. It started with the move, about fourteen months ago, and instead of settling down once we got unpacked, the worrying just sort of... expanded. It latched onto the move first, and then it just kept finding new things to attach itself to, right up until today.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days where you felt down, hopeless, or lost interest in things you usually enjoy, for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks at a time?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I mean, I wouldn't say I feel hopeless or down in the dumps like that. It's not a sadness, if that makes sense. It's more of a... a constant buzzing energy, but not the good kind of energy. It's that nervous, on-edge feeling, like I'm waiting for the other shoe to drop all the time. \n\nI certainly haven't lost interest in things. So, no, I haven't lost interest in my life or felt hopeless for weeks on end. It’s just that I can’t seem to enjoy any of those things fully because there's always this underlying current of worry running through my mind while I'm doing them. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Fatigued? You know, it's funny you ask that, because my husband, Richard, was just saying the other day that he doesn't know how I keep going at the pace I do. So, no, I wouldn't say I feel fatigued or low on energy at all. If anything, it's the exact opposite. Like I was saying before about that buzzing feeling? It's like I have too much energy, but it's all just... nervous energy. 

I'll be up at six in the morning, making coffee, pulling up the MLS listings on my iPad to see what went active overnight or what had a price drop, and from there I'm just... go, go, go. I'll run over to the condo association meeting—because let me tell you, if you don't stay on top of those people, they'll pass a special assessment for landscaping before you can even blink—and then I'm off showing houses all afternoon. 

But even when I haven't done anything taxing, like if I actually try to just sit down on a Sunday afternoon and watch a movie with Richard or read a book... I can't just sit there. My body might be resting, but my mind is running an absolute marathon, and I just end up getting up and reorganizing the hall closet or drafting emails to my clients for Monday morning. I'm just constantly revved up. Honestly, sometimes I wish I *could* feel a little fatigued, just so I could finally sit still for five minutes without feeling like I need to be doing something or checking on something. So, no, definitely no lack of energy here. Just the opposite, really.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yes, actually. That is something I’ve really been noticing lately, and it’s incredibly frustrating because, in my line of work, you absolutely cannot afford to miss a detail. Real estate is all about the details—the dates, the contingencies, the disclosures. But lately, with all this constant worrying I was telling you about, it’s like my brain just gets hijacked. 

For instance, just the other day I was sitting at my desk—well, my new desk at the condo, which I’m still trying to get organized because the lighting in that room is completely different from my old office—and I was trying to review a purchase agreement for my clients, the Millers. They’re buying this lovely mid-century modern place, but the inspection came back with some issues on the HVAC system, so I was drafting the inspection addendum. And I swear to you, I sat there and read the exact same paragraph three or four times. I’d start reading, and then suddenly I’m thinking, "Did I schedule the termite inspector for Tuesday or Wednesday?" or "What if the sellers completely reject this addendum and the whole deal falls apart and the Millers lose their earnest money?" 

And before I know it, I’m at the bottom of the page and I have absolutely no idea what I just read. My mind just goes completely blank on the task at hand because this other worry just barges in and takes over. I lose the thread entirely. I have to physically stop, take a breath, and force myself back to the top of the page. It’s exhausting, to be honest. I’ve always prided myself on being sharp and catching every little thing in these contracts, but now it takes me twice as long to get through a simple document because I just can't keep my focus where it needs to be. The worry just pulls me right out of it.

And I mean everything. \n\nI’m a real estate broker, and, you know, you always have a little stress with the job, but lately my worry about my clients and the housing market—which, granted, is a bit all over the place right now with the rates—but my reaction to it just feels completely out of proportion to what is actually happening. I know it’s too much, I know I shouldn't be this wound up over things that are mostly fine, but my brain just won't let it go and I just don't know how to turn it off anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any recurring thoughts that you can’t seem to get out of your head, or do you find yourself doing certain things over and over again to try to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: No, not exactly. I mean, I don't have one specific thought that just loops like a broken record, and I certainly don't do things over and over again to make it stop. I know some people have to, you know, check the locks a dozen times or wash their hands constantly, but it’s nothing like that for me. It’s not a specific action I have to perform. \n\nAlthough, I suppose my husband would say I check my phone for work emails too much, but honestly, in real estate, especially right now, if you miss a text from a buyer's agent, you could lose the whole deal, so that's just being responsible. That's just the job. But as for the thoughts... it's not one single recurring thought, it's more like a domino effect. It just travels from one thing to the next. \n\nLike with the condo, I'll start out wondering if we got a good interest rate on the mortgage, and then suddenly I'm worrying about the building's HOA reserves, and then that makes me worry about our own personal retirement accounts, and then I'm stressing over whether my daughter is saving enough in her 401k... it just spirals. It’s never just one thought stuck in my head, it's a whole parade of different worries. And I don't do anything to try and reduce it because, well, I don't even know what I *could* do to stop it. It just keeps flowing from one topic to another all day long, completely on its own.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any physical symptoms like restlessness, trouble sleeping, muscle tension, or irritability that seem to go along with all this worrying?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, actually. The sleeping part is... it's just awful. You know, I try to do everything right. I make sure I'm in bed at a reasonable hour, usually by 10:30 or 11:00, because I know how important rest is, especially at my age, and especially when you have to be \"on\" for clients all day. But getting into bed and actually sleeping are two very different things for me lately. \n\nI’ll lay there, and it’s like my brain just clicks into overdrive the second the lights go out. I’ll start thinking about the condo fees—because they just raised them again, and I wonder if they're going to keep going up and if we should have factored that in more—and then my mind jumps to the showings I have the next day. I'll start mentally walking through the properties, wondering if the staging is right, if I should have brought fresh flowers for the kitchen island, if I missed some tiny clause in a contract I sent over for signatures earlier that afternoon... \n\nIt just circles and circles, and I'm lying there staring at the ceiling for what feels like hours. It takes me so long to finally settle down and drift off. And even when I do finally fall asleep, I don't think it's good sleep, you know? Because when my alarm goes off, I wake up feeling completely drained, like I never fully rested at all. I drag myself out of bed feeling just as exhausted as when I got in it. It’s like my brain is running a marathon while I’m supposed to be recharging. So yes, the sleep is definitely a big problem.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with constant worrying and trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think... it's been quite a while now. I'd say it really started about a year and two months ago. Yes, right around then. That's when we finally sold our house—the one we'd lived in for over thirty years, where we raised our daughter, you know? It was a beautiful place, a big colonial, but it was just getting to be too much upkeep for the two of us, especially with my husband's knees acting up, and the yard work was endless. \n\nSo we decided to downsize and move into this condo. Which, at the time, seemed like the most sensible thing in the world. We found a lovely unit, great natural light, close to downtown... but almost the minute the ink was dry on the closing papers, this whole... cloud of worry just descended on me. \n\nI remember the first few nights in the new place, surrounded by all those boxes. I should have been relieved that the move was over, but instead, I was just lying awake, staring at an unfamiliar ceiling, second-guessing everything. Did we get enough for the house? Did we overpay for the condo? Are the neighbors going to be noisy? What if the building has structural issues they didn't disclose? And it just never stopped. It started with the move, about fourteen months ago, and instead of settling down once we got unpacked, the worrying just sort of... expanded. It latched onto the move first, and then it just kept finding new things to attach itself to, right up until today.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days where you felt down, hopeless, or lost interest in things you usually enjoy, for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks at a time?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I mean, I wouldn't say I feel hopeless or down in the dumps like that. It's not a sadness, if that makes sense. It's more of a... a constant buzzing energy, but not the good kind of energy. It's that nervous, on-edge feeling, like I'm waiting for the other shoe to drop all the time. \n\nI certainly haven't lost interest in things. I still love my job, even though the market is just completely unpredictable right now—one minute houses are sitting for sixty days, and the next, there's a bidding war on a complete fixer-upper. I still enjoy taking my clients out, showing them properties, negotiating the deals. In fact, sometimes I think I throw myself into the work *too* much because it gives my brain something specific to focus on, even if I end up worrying about the contracts later. \n\nAnd I still love seeing my daughter, of course, and going out to dinner with my husband. We have this little Italian place we go to every Friday night, and I still look forward to that. We've been going there for fifteen years, they know exactly how I like my chicken piccata... So, no, I haven't lost interest in my life or felt hopeless for weeks on end. It’s just that I can’t seem to enjoy any of those things fully because there's always this underlying current of worry running through my mind while I'm doing them. I'm sitting there at dinner, eating my chicken, and part of my brain is wondering if I remembered to lock the patio door at the condo, or if the HOA is going to send out another special assessment notice. But no, no deep depression or hopelessness like you're describing. Just the worrying.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you feel anxious or worried most days, even when there isn’t a specific problem or stressor happening at that moment?\n\nPatient: Well, yes. Yes, I do. That's exactly why I'm here, actually, because it feels like I worry about absolutely everything now, even when I'm just sitting on the sofa and nothing is technically wrong. It's just constant. I'll be sitting there, and suddenly I'm worrying about my clients, you know? Did I price that new listing correctly? Should we have painted the trim before putting it on the market? And then that spirals into worrying about the real estate market in general, because interest rates are doing whatever they're doing, and who knows if buyers are going to dry up completely by spring. \n\nAnd then, if it's not work, it's my family. I worry about my daughter, even though she's thirty-two and perfectly capable, but I'll wonder if she's saving enough for retirement or if she's working too many hours. And money—oh, I worry about money constantly, even though we made a good profit on the house. I just sit there and think, what if inflation eats away at our savings? What if my husband's knees get worse and he needs surgery and Medicare doesn't cover all the physical therapy? \n\nAnd of course, there's the condo. I'm still second-guessing if moving was the right decision, even on a quiet Sunday morning when nothing is happening. I'll just be drinking my coffee, and my mind is racing through all these scenarios, and... and I know, logically, that it's out of proportion. I know I'm making mountains out of molehills, but I just can't seem to stop the wheels from turning. It's like my brain is always searching for a problem to solve, even when there isn't one right in front of me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel fatigued or low on energy, even when you haven’t been doing anything particularly taxing?\n\nPatient: Fatigued? You know, it's funny you ask that, because my husband, Richard, was just saying the other day that he doesn't know how I keep going at the pace I do. So, no, I wouldn't say I feel fatigued or low on energy at all. If anything, it's the exact opposite. Like I was saying before about that buzzing feeling? It's like I have too much energy, but it's all just... nervous energy. \n\nI'll be up at six in the morning, making coffee, pulling up the MLS listings on my iPad to see what went active overnight or what had a price drop, and from there I'm just... go, go, go. I'll run over to the condo association meeting—because let me tell you, if you don't stay on top of those people, they'll pass a special assessment for landscaping before you can even blink—and then I'm off showing houses all afternoon. \n\nBut even when I haven't done anything taxing, like if I actually try to just sit down on a Sunday afternoon and watch a movie with Richard or read a book... I can't just sit there. My body might be resting, but my mind is running an absolute marathon, and I just end up getting up and reorganizing the hall closet or drafting emails to my clients for Monday morning. I'm just constantly revved up. Honestly, sometimes I wish I *could* feel a little fatigued, just so I could finally sit still for five minutes without feeling like I need to be doing something or checking on something. So, no, definitely no lack of energy here. Just the opposite, really.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any trouble focusing or concentrating, like your mind going blank or being unable to stay on task because of all the worrying?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, actually. That is something I’ve really been noticing lately, and it’s incredibly frustrating because, in my line of work, you absolutely cannot afford to miss a detail. Real estate is all about the details—the dates, the contingencies, the disclosures. But lately, with all this constant worrying I was telling you about, it’s like my brain just gets hijacked. \n\nFor instance, just the other day I was sitting at my desk—well, my new desk at the condo, which I’m still trying to get organized because the lighting in that room is completely different from my old office—and I was trying to review a purchase agreement for my clients, the Millers. They’re buying this lovely mid-century modern place, but the inspection came back with some issues on the HVAC system, so I was drafting the inspection addendum. And I swear to you, I sat there and read the exact same paragraph three or four times. I’d start reading, and then suddenly I’m thinking, \"Did I schedule the termite inspector for Tuesday or Wednesday?\" or \"What if the sellers completely reject this addendum and the whole deal falls apart and the Millers lose their earnest money?\" \n\nAnd before I know it, I’m at the bottom of the page and I have absolutely no idea what I just read. My mind just goes completely blank on the task at hand because this other worry just barges in and takes over. I lose the thread entirely. I have to physically stop, take a breath, and force myself back to the top of the page. It’s exhausting, to be honest. I’ve always prided myself on being sharp and catching every little thing in these contracts, but now it takes me twice as long to get through a simple document because I just can't keep my focus where it needs to be. The worry just pulls me right out of it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I can't say I've ever had anything quite like that. Sudden episodes of intense fear? No, it's not like that at all. It's not a sudden spike or a panic, you know, where I feel like I'm losing control or can't breathe. I've heard of people having those—my sister-in-law, actually, had a terrible time with them after her divorce, said she felt like she was having a heart attack right in the middle of the grocery store by the produce section. But for me, it's never been like that. 

My heart doesn't suddenly start pounding out of my chest, and I don't get that suffocating feeling. It's more of a... well, like I was saying before, it's a constant, low-level hum of worry. It's always there, always churning in the background. It's not a sudden attack that comes out of nowhere and knocks me off my feet; it's just this steady stream of "what ifs" that I can't seem to turn off. Like, I'll be driving to a showing, and instead of just listening to the radio, I'm running through every possible thing that could go wrong with the appraisal, or wondering if I remembered to lock the back door at the condo, or worrying if Richard remembered to take his blood pressure medication. 

So, no, I wouldn't describe it as sudden or intense fear. I'm always very much in control, maybe too in control, honestly, because I'm trying to manage all these potential disasters in my head before they even happen. It's just exhausting because it never really peaks and goes away; it just stays with me all day long. But no, no racing heart or feeling like I can't breathe or anything like that. Just the constant, never-ending worry.

It’s never just one thought stuck in my head, it's a whole parade of different worries. And I don't do anything to try and reduce it because, well, I don't even know what I *could* do to stop it. It just keeps flowing from one topic to another all day long, completely on its own.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any physical symptoms like restlessness, trouble sleeping, muscle tension, or irritability that seem to go along with all this worrying?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, actually. The sleeping part is... it's just awful. You know, I try to do everything right. It takes me so long to finally settle down and drift off. And even when I do finally fall asleep, I don't think it's good sleep, you know? It’s like my brain is running a marathon while I’m supposed to be recharging. So yes, the sleep is definitely a big problem.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with constant worrying and trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think... it's been quite a while now. It started with the move, about fourteen months ago, and instead of settling down once we got unpacked, the worrying just sort of... expanded. It latched onto the move first, and then it just kept finding new things to attach itself to, right up until today.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days where you felt down, hopeless, or lost interest in things you usually enjoy, for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks at a time?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I mean, I wouldn't say I feel hopeless or down in the dumps like that. It's not a sadness, if that makes sense. It's more of a... a constant buzzing energy, but not the good kind of energy. It was a beautiful place, a big colonial, but it was just getting to be too much upkeep for the two of us, especially with my husband's knees acting up, and the yard work was endless. \n\nSo we decided to downsize and move into this condo. Which, at the time, seemed like the most sensible thing in the world. We found a lovely unit, great natural light, close to downtown... but almost the minute the ink was dry on the closing papers, this whole... cloud of worry just descended on me. \n\nI remember the first few nights in the new place, surrounded by all those boxes. I should have been relieved that the move was over, but instead, I was just lying awake, staring at an unfamiliar ceiling, second-guessing everything. Did we get enough for the house? Did we overpay for the condo? Are the neighbors going to be noisy? What if the building has structural issues they didn't disclose? And it just never stopped. It started with the move, about fourteen months ago, and instead of settling down once we got unpacked, the worrying just sort of... expanded. It latched onto the move first, and then it just kept finding new things to attach itself to, right up until today.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days where you felt down, hopeless, or lost interest in things you usually enjoy, for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks at a time?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I mean, I wouldn't say I feel hopeless or down in the dumps like that. It's not a sadness, if that makes sense. It's more of a... a constant buzzing energy, but not the good kind of energy. It's that nervous, on-edge feeling, like I'm waiting for the other shoe to drop all the time. \n\nI certainly haven't lost interest in things. I still love my job, even though the market is just completely unpredictable right now—one minute houses are sitting for sixty days, and the next, there's a bidding war on a complete fixer-upper. I still enjoy taking my clients out, showing them properties, negotiating the deals. In fact, sometimes I think I throw myself into the work *too* much because it gives my brain something specific to focus on, even if I end up worrying about the contracts later. \n\nAnd I still love seeing my daughter, of course, and going out to dinner with my husband. We have this little Italian place we go to every Friday night, and I still look forward to that. We've been going there for fifteen years, they know exactly how I like my chicken piccata... So, no, I haven't lost interest in my life or felt hopeless for weeks on end. It’s just that I can’t seem to enjoy any of those things fully because there's always this underlying current of worry running through my mind while I'm doing them. I'm sitting there at dinner, eating my chicken, and part of my brain is wondering if I remembered to lock the patio door at the condo, or if the HOA is going to send out another special assessment notice. But no, no deep depression or hopelessness like you're describing. Just the worrying.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you feel anxious or worried most days, even when there isn’t a specific problem or stressor happening at that moment?\n\nPatient: Well, yes. Yes, I do. That's exactly why I'm here, actually, because it feels like I worry about absolutely everything now, even when I'm just sitting on the sofa and nothing is technically wrong. It's just constant. I'll be sitting there, and suddenly I'm worrying about my clients, you know? Did I price that new listing correctly? Should we have painted the trim before putting it on the market? And then that spirals into worrying about the real estate market in general, because interest rates are doing whatever they're doing, and who knows if buyers are going to dry up completely by spring. \n\nAnd then, if it's not work, it's my family. I worry about my daughter, even though she's thirty-two and perfectly capable, but I'll wonder if she's saving enough for retirement or if she's working too many hours. And money—oh, I worry about money constantly, even though we made a good profit on the house. I just sit there and think, what if inflation eats away at our savings? What if my husband's knees get worse and he needs surgery and Medicare doesn't cover all the physical therapy? \n\nAnd of course, there's the condo. I'm still second-guessing if moving was the right decision, even on a quiet Sunday morning when nothing is happening. I'll just be drinking my coffee, and my mind is racing through all these scenarios, and... and I know, logically, that it's out of proportion. I know I'm making mountains out of molehills, but I just can't seem to stop the wheels from turning. It's like my brain is always searching for a problem to solve, even when there isn't one right in front of me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel fatigued or low on energy, even when you haven’t been doing anything particularly taxing?\n\nPatient: Fatigued? You know, it's funny you ask that, because my husband, Richard, was just saying the other day that he doesn't know how I keep going at the pace I do. So, no, I wouldn't say I feel fatigued or low on energy at all. If anything, it's the exact opposite. Like I was saying before about that buzzing feeling? It's like I have too much energy, but it's all just... nervous energy. \n\nI'll be up at six in the morning, making coffee, pulling up the MLS listings on my iPad to see what went active overnight or what had a price drop, and from there I'm just... go, go, go. I'll run over to the condo association meeting—because let me tell you, if you don't stay on top of those people, they'll pass a special assessment for landscaping before you can even blink—and then I'm off showing houses all afternoon. \n\nBut even when I haven't done anything taxing, like if I actually try to just sit down on a Sunday afternoon and watch a movie with Richard or read a book... I can't just sit there. My body might be resting, but my mind is running an absolute marathon, and I just end up getting up and reorganizing the hall closet or drafting emails to my clients for Monday morning. I'm just constantly revved up. Honestly, sometimes I wish I *could* feel a little fatigued, just so I could finally sit still for five minutes without feeling like I need to be doing something or checking on something. So, no, definitely no lack of energy here. Just the opposite, really.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any trouble focusing or concentrating, like your mind going blank or being unable to stay on task because of all the worrying?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, actually. That is something I’ve really been noticing lately, and it’s incredibly frustrating because, in my line of work, you absolutely cannot afford to miss a detail. Real estate is all about the details—the dates, the contingencies, the disclosures. But lately, with all this constant worrying I was telling you about, it’s like my brain just gets hijacked. \n\nFor instance, just the other day I was sitting at my desk—well, my new desk at the condo, which I’m still trying to get organized because the lighting in that room is completely different from my old office—and I was trying to review a purchase agreement for my clients, the Millers. They’re buying this lovely mid-century modern place, but the inspection came back with some issues on the HVAC system, so I was drafting the inspection addendum. And I swear to you, I sat there and read the exact same paragraph three or four times. I’d start reading, and then suddenly I’m thinking, \"Did I schedule the termite inspector for Tuesday or Wednesday?\" or \"What if the sellers completely reject this addendum and the whole deal falls apart and the Millers lose their earnest money?\" \n\nAnd before I know it, I’m at the bottom of the page and I have absolutely no idea what I just read. My mind just goes completely blank on the task at hand because this other worry just barges in and takes over. I lose the thread entirely. I have to physically stop, take a breath, and force myself back to the top of the page. It’s exhausting, to be honest. I’ve always prided myself on being sharp and catching every little thing in these contracts, but now it takes me twice as long to get through a simple document because I just can't keep my focus where it needs to be. The worry just pulls me right out of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced sudden episodes of intense fear or discomfort, where you felt like your heart was racing, you couldn't breathe, or you were losing control?\n\nPatient: No, I can't say I've ever had anything quite like that. Sudden episodes of intense fear? No, it's not like that at all. It's not a sudden spike or a panic, you know, where I feel like I'm losing control or can't breathe. I've heard of people having those—my sister-in-law, actually, had a terrible time with them after her divorce, said she felt like she was having a heart attack right in the middle of the grocery store by the produce section. But for me, it's never been like that. \n\nMy heart doesn't suddenly start pounding out of my chest, and I don't get that suffocating feeling. It's more of a... well, like I was saying before, it's a constant, low-level hum of worry. It's always there, always churning in the background. It's not a sudden attack that comes out of nowhere and knocks me off my feet; it's just this steady stream of \"what ifs\" that I can't seem to turn off. Like, I'll be driving to a showing, and instead of just listening to the radio, I'm running through every possible thing that could go wrong with the appraisal, or wondering if I remembered to lock the back door at the condo, or worrying if Richard remembered to take his blood pressure medication. \n\nSo, no, I wouldn't describe it as sudden or intense fear. I'm always very much in control, maybe too in control, honestly, because I'm trying to manage all these potential disasters in my head before they even happen. It's just exhausting because it never really peaks and goes away; it just stays with me all day long. But no, no racing heart or feeling like I can't breathe or anything like that. Just the constant, never-ending worry.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Irritable? Oh, well... I mean, I suppose Richard might say so, but I don't really think I'm any more irritable than anyone else would be in my shoes. I mean, if you want to talk about being easily annoyed, you have to look at what I'm dealing with on a daily basis. 

Just yesterday, for example, I was trying to coordinate a final walk-through for a property over on Elm Street—a beautiful colonial, completely updated kitchen, but the sellers are just dragging their feet on moving their things out. And I'm sitting there, my mind is already spinning about whether the title company has the updated payoff statement, and whether the buyer's lender is going to clear the file to close on time, and then my phone rings. And it's the listing agent, telling me they need another day to get the garage cleaned out. 

Well, I just... I completely snapped at him. I told him that a contract is a contract and they can't just change the terms at the eleventh hour because they didn't plan their packing properly. And after I hung up, I did feel a little bad, because usually I'm very professional, very diplomatic. But it's like, when my brain is already juggling fifteen different "what ifs" and I'm so worried about everything falling apart, my patience is just... it's just nonexistent. 

And it happens with Richard, too, bless his heart. He'll ask me something simple, like what we should have for dinner, while I'm in the middle of worrying about an inspection report, and I'll just bite his head off. I'll say, "I can't possibly think about chicken versus fish right now, Richard, the entire plumbing system at the Oakwood house might need to be replaced!" So... I guess, yes, maybe I am a bit more irritable lately. But it's not like I'm just walking around angry for no reason. It's just that when I'm so overwhelmed by all the things that could go wrong, any little interruption or extra problem just feels like the straw that breaks the camel's back, you know? I just don't have the bandwidth for it.

I'm Dr. [Name]. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi, Dr. [Name]. Thank you for fitting me in. Well, I came in today because... gosh, where do I even start? I just seem to worry about absolutely everything now. And I mean everything. \n\nI’m a real estate broker, and, you know, you always have a little stress with the job, but lately my worry about my clients and the housing market—which, granted, is a bit all over the place right now with the rates—but my reaction to it just feels completely out of proportion to what is actually happening. I know the business, I’ve been doing this a long time, but I just can't stop fretting over every little detail. \n\nAnd it’s not just work, it spills over into everything else. I worry about my family constantly, I worry about money even though we're okay, it's... oh, even this condo! We recently moved to a condo, downsizing, you know, which was supposed to make life simpler, but I just agonize over whether it was the right decision. I'll sit there and think, 'Did we make a mistake? What if it doesn't appreciate? Did we sell the house at the wrong time?' It’s just exhausting. I know it’s too much, I know I shouldn't be this wound up over things that are mostly fine, but my brain just won't let it go and I just don't know how to turn it off anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any recurring thoughts that you can’t seem to get out of your head, or do you find yourself doing certain things over and over again to try to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: No, not exactly. I mean, I don't have one specific thought that just loops like a broken record, and I certainly don't do things over and over again to make it stop. I know some people have to, you know, check the locks a dozen times or wash their hands constantly, but it’s nothing like that for me. It’s not a specific action I have to perform. \n\nAlthough, I suppose my husband would say I check my phone for work emails too much, but honestly, in real estate, especially right now, if you miss a text from a buyer's agent, you could lose the whole deal, so that's just being responsible. That's just the job. But as for the thoughts... it's not one single recurring thought, it's more like a domino effect. It just travels from one thing to the next. \n\nLike with the condo, I'll start out wondering if we got a good interest rate on the mortgage, and then suddenly I'm worrying about the building's HOA reserves, and then that makes me worry about our own personal retirement accounts, and then I'm stressing over whether my daughter is saving enough in her 401k... it just spirals. It’s never just one thought stuck in my head, it's a whole parade of different worries. And I don't do anything to try and reduce it because, well, I don't even know what I *could* do to stop it. It just keeps flowing from one topic to another all day long, completely on its own.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any physical symptoms like restlessness, trouble sleeping, muscle tension, or irritability that seem to go along with all this worrying?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, actually. The sleeping part is... it's just awful. You know, I try to do everything right. I make sure I'm in bed at a reasonable hour, usually by 10:30 or 11:00, because I know how important rest is, especially at my age, and especially when you have to be \"on\" for clients all day. But getting into bed and actually sleeping are two very different things for me lately. \n\nI’ll lay there, and it’s like my brain just clicks into overdrive the second the lights go out. I’ll start thinking about the condo fees—because they just raised them again, and I wonder if they're going to keep going up and if we should have factored that in more—and then my mind jumps to the showings I have the next day. I'll start mentally walking through the properties, wondering if the staging is right, if I should have brought fresh flowers for the kitchen island, if I missed some tiny clause in a contract I sent over for signatures earlier that afternoon... \n\nIt just circles and circles, and I'm lying there staring at the ceiling for what feels like hours. It takes me so long to finally settle down and drift off. And even when I do finally fall asleep, I don't think it's good sleep, you know? Because when my alarm goes off, I wake up feeling completely drained, like I never fully rested at all. I drag myself out of bed feeling just as exhausted as when I got in it. It’s like my brain is running a marathon while I’m supposed to be recharging. So yes, the sleep is definitely a big problem.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with constant worrying and trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think... it's been quite a while now. I'd say it really started about a year and two months ago. Yes, right around then. That's when we finally sold our house—the one we'd lived in for over thirty years, where we raised our daughter, you know? It was a beautiful place, a big colonial, but it was just getting to be too much upkeep for the two of us, especially with my husband's knees acting up, and the yard work was endless. \n\nSo we decided to downsize and move into this condo. Which, at the time, seemed like the most sensible thing in the world. We found a lovely unit, great natural light, close to downtown... but almost the minute the ink was dry on the closing papers, this whole... cloud of worry just descended on me. \n\nI remember the first few nights in the new place, surrounded by all those boxes. I should have been relieved that the move was over, but instead, I was just lying awake, staring at an unfamiliar ceiling, second-guessing everything. Did we get enough for the house? Did we overpay for the condo? Are the neighbors going to be noisy? What if the building has structural issues they didn't disclose? And it just never stopped. It started with the move, about fourteen months ago, and instead of settling down once we got unpacked, the worrying just sort of... expanded. It latched onto the move first, and then it just kept finding new things to attach itself to, right up until today.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days where you felt down, hopeless, or lost interest in things you usually enjoy, for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks at a time?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I mean, I wouldn't say I feel hopeless or down in the dumps like that. It's not a sadness, if that makes sense. It's more of a... a constant buzzing energy, but not the good kind of energy. It's that nervous, on-edge feeling, like I'm waiting for the other shoe to drop all the time. \n\nI certainly haven't lost interest in things. I still love my job, even though the market is just completely unpredictable right now—one minute houses are sitting for sixty days, and the next, there's a bidding war on a complete fixer-upper. I still enjoy taking my clients out, showing them properties, negotiating the deals. In fact, sometimes I think I throw myself into the work *too* much because it gives my brain something specific to focus on, even if I end up worrying about the contracts later. \n\nAnd I still love seeing my daughter, of course, and going out to dinner with my husband. We have this little Italian place we go to every Friday night, and I still look forward to that. We've been going there for fifteen years, they know exactly how I like my chicken piccata... So, no, I haven't lost interest in my life or felt hopeless for weeks on end. It’s just that I can’t seem to enjoy any of those things fully because there's always this underlying current of worry running through my mind while I'm doing them. I'm sitting there at dinner, eating my chicken, and part of my brain is wondering if I remembered to lock the patio door at the condo, or if the HOA is going to send out another special assessment notice. But no, no deep depression or hopelessness like you're describing. Just the worrying.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you feel anxious or worried most days, even when there isn’t a specific problem or stressor happening at that moment?\n\nPatient: Well, yes. Yes, I do. That's exactly why I'm here, actually, because it feels like I worry about absolutely everything now, even when I'm just sitting on the sofa and nothing is technically wrong. It's just constant. I'll be sitting there, and suddenly I'm worrying about my clients, you know? Did I price that new listing correctly? Should we have painted the trim before putting it on the market? And then that spirals into worrying about the real estate market in general, because interest rates are doing whatever they're doing, and who knows if buyers are going to dry up completely by spring. \n\nAnd then, if it's not work, it's my family. I worry about my daughter, even though she's thirty-two and perfectly capable, but I'll wonder if she's saving enough for retirement or if she's working too many hours. And money—oh, I worry about money constantly, even though we made a good profit on the house. I just sit there and think, what if inflation eats away at our savings? What if my husband's knees get worse and he needs surgery and Medicare doesn't cover all the physical therapy? \n\nAnd of course, there's the condo. I'm still second-guessing if moving was the right decision, even on a quiet Sunday morning when nothing is happening. I'll just be drinking my coffee, and my mind is racing through all these scenarios, and... and I know, logically, that it's out of proportion. I know I'm making mountains out of molehills, but I just can't seem to stop the wheels from turning. It's like my brain is always searching for a problem to solve, even when there isn't one right in front of me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel fatigued or low on energy, even when you haven’t been doing anything particularly taxing?\n\nPatient: Fatigued? You know, it's funny you ask that, because my husband, Richard, was just saying the other day that he doesn't know how I keep going at the pace I do. So, no, I wouldn't say I feel fatigued or low on energy at all. If anything, it's the exact opposite. Like I was saying before about that buzzing feeling? It's like I have too much energy, but it's all just... nervous energy. \n\nI'll be up at six in the morning, making coffee, pulling up the MLS listings on my iPad to see what went active overnight or what had a price drop, and from there I'm just... go, go, go. I'll run over to the condo association meeting—because let me tell you, if you don't stay on top of those people, they'll pass a special assessment for landscaping before you can even blink—and then I'm off showing houses all afternoon. \n\nBut even when I haven't done anything taxing, like if I actually try to just sit down on a Sunday afternoon and watch a movie with Richard or read a book... I can't just sit there. My body might be resting, but my mind is running an absolute marathon, and I just end up getting up and reorganizing the hall closet or drafting emails to my clients for Monday morning. I'm just constantly revved up. Honestly, sometimes I wish I *could* feel a little fatigued, just so I could finally sit still for five minutes without feeling like I need to be doing something or checking on something. So, no, definitely no lack of energy here. Just the opposite, really.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any trouble focusing or concentrating, like your mind going blank or being unable to stay on task because of all the worrying?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, actually. That is something I’ve really been noticing lately, and it’s incredibly frustrating because, in my line of work, you absolutely cannot afford to miss a detail. Real estate is all about the details—the dates, the contingencies, the disclosures. But lately, with all this constant worrying I was telling you about, it’s like my brain just gets hijacked. \n\nFor instance, just the other day I was sitting at my desk—well, my new desk at the condo, which I’m still trying to get organized because the lighting in that room is completely different from my old office—and I was trying to review a purchase agreement for my clients, the Millers. They’re buying this lovely mid-century modern place, but the inspection came back with some issues on the HVAC system, so I was drafting the inspection addendum. And I swear to you, I sat there and read the exact same paragraph three or four times. I’d start reading, and then suddenly I’m thinking, \"Did I schedule the termite inspector for Tuesday or Wednesday?\" or \"What if the sellers completely reject this addendum and the whole deal falls apart and the Millers lose their earnest money?\" \n\nAnd before I know it, I’m at the bottom of the page and I have absolutely no idea what I just read. My mind just goes completely blank on the task at hand because this other worry just barges in and takes over. I lose the thread entirely. I have to physically stop, take a breath, and force myself back to the top of the page. It’s exhausting, to be honest. I’ve always prided myself on being sharp and catching every little thing in these contracts, but now it takes me twice as long to get through a simple document because I just can't keep my focus where it needs to be. The worry just pulls me right out of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced sudden episodes of intense fear or discomfort, where you felt like your heart was racing, you couldn't breathe, or you were losing control?\n\nPatient: No, I can't say I've ever had anything quite like that. Sudden episodes of intense fear? No, it's not like that at all. It's not a sudden spike or a panic, you know, where I feel like I'm losing control or can't breathe. I've heard of people having those—my sister-in-law, actually, had a terrible time with them after her divorce, said she felt like she was having a heart attack right in the middle of the grocery store by the produce section. But for me, it's never been like that. \n\nMy heart doesn't suddenly start pounding out of my chest, and I don't get that suffocating feeling. It's more of a... well, like I was saying before, it's a constant, low-level hum of worry. It's always there, always churning in the background. It's not a sudden attack that comes out of nowhere and knocks me off my feet; it's just this steady stream of \"what ifs\" that I can't seem to turn off. Like, I'll be driving to a showing, and instead of just listening to the radio, I'm running through every possible thing that could go wrong with the appraisal, or wondering if I remembered to lock the back door at the condo, or worrying if Richard remembered to take his blood pressure medication. \n\nSo, no, I wouldn't describe it as sudden or intense fear. I'm always very much in control, maybe too in control, honestly, because I'm trying to manage all these potential disasters in my head before they even happen. It's just exhausting because it never really peaks and goes away; it just stays with me all day long. But no, no racing heart or feeling like I can't breathe or anything like that. Just the constant, never-ending worry.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself becoming more irritable or easily annoyed, especially when you're feeling overwhelmed by all the worrying?\n\nPatient: Irritable? Oh, well... I mean, I suppose Richard might say so, but I don't really think I'm any more irritable than anyone else would be in my shoes. I mean, if you want to talk about being easily annoyed, you have to look at what I'm dealing with on a daily basis. \n\nJust yesterday, for example, I was trying to coordinate a final walk-through for a property over on Elm Street—a beautiful colonial, completely updated kitchen, but the sellers are just dragging their feet on moving their things out. And I'm sitting there, my mind is already spinning about whether the title company has the updated payoff statement, and whether the buyer's lender is going to clear the file to close on time, and then my phone rings. And it's the listing agent, telling me they need another day to get the garage cleaned out. \n\nWell, I just... I completely snapped at him. I told him that a contract is a contract and they can't just change the terms at the eleventh hour because they didn't plan their packing properly. And after I hung up, I did feel a little bad, because usually I'm very professional, very diplomatic. But it's like, when my brain is already juggling fifteen different \"what ifs\" and I'm so worried about everything falling apart, my patience is just... it's just nonexistent. \n\nAnd it happens with Richard, too, bless his heart. He'll ask me something simple, like what we should have for dinner, while I'm in the middle of worrying about an inspection report, and I'll just bite his head off. I'll say, \"I can't possibly think about chicken versus fish right now, Richard, the entire plumbing system at the Oakwood house might need to be replaced!\" So... I guess, yes, maybe I am a bit more irritable lately. But it's not like I'm just walking around angry for no reason. It's just that when I'm so overwhelmed by all the things that could go wrong, any little interruption or extra problem just feels like the straw that breaks the camel's back, you know? I just don't have the bandwidth for it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Sweating or trembling? No, not really. It’s kind of like what we were talking about earlier with the sudden panic—I just don't get those physical reactions. I mean, my mind will be going a million miles a minute, but my body doesn't really do all of that shaking or sweating. 

I actually had the perfect scenario for that just last Sunday, over in the Heritage subdivision. I was hosting an open house for this gorgeous four-bedroom craftsman. The sellers, lovely people, but they have three golden retrievers, so I had spent the entire morning just making sure there wasn't a single dog hair in sight, spraying linen mist, getting the lighting just right. And literally ten minutes before the open house is supposed to start, I realize the air conditioning isn't kicking on. It’s the middle of the afternoon, the sun is beating down on the big bay windows, and the thermostat is just climbing. 

Now, my mind immediately started spiraling. I’m thinking, "The buyers are going to walk in, they’re going to feel the heat, they’re going to assume the HVAC is completely shot, they’re going to think the house is a money pit, and this listing is going to sit on the market for months." I was worrying about having to explain to the sellers why we didn't get any offers, worrying about whether I should try to call an emergency repairman on a Sunday, which costs an absolute fortune. 

But even with all of that intense worry just flooding my brain, I wasn't physically shaking. My hands weren't trembling when I was setting out the sign-in sheets, and my heart wasn't racing. I mean, I might have been a little warm because the house was literally heating up, but I wasn't in a cold sweat or having nervous palpitations or anything like that. I was perfectly steady on the outside, greeting people at the door with a smile, handing out brochures. You would never look at me and think, "Oh, she's trembling," or "She looks shaky." It’s all entirely internal. It’s just that relentless mental churning, not my body actually shaking or my heart pounding out of my chest. So, no, I haven't really noticed any of those physical things you mentioned.
